```text
1	\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}
2	
3	Table~\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.
4	All results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.
5	
6	\begin{table}[ht]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}
10	\label{tab:instruct}
11	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}
13	\toprule
14	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Binary F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\boldsymbol{\rho}$\textbf{(ent.\ mean)}} & \\
15	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
16	\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \textbf{Rev.} \\
17	\midrule
18	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
19	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
20	Llama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	Instruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\Delta| \leq 3.2$\,pp).
26	The asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.
27	Entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\ 3 of 7 for Llama.
28	This pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}
31	To test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).
32	
33	\begin{table}[ht]
34	\centering
35	\small
36	\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}
37	\label{tab:instruct-retrain}
38	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
39	\toprule
40	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Base$\rightarrow$Inst} & \textbf{Retrained} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
41	\midrule
42	OLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\
43	Qwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\
44	Qwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	Retraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).
50	Three-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\,=\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.
51	
52	\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}
53	$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\,=\,0.338 (vs.\ 0.632 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.
54	The compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).
55	Cross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\,=\,0.490; instruct$\rightarrow$base F1\,=\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.
56	These results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions.
57	
58	\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}
59	To test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).
60	
61	\begin{table}[ht]
62	\centering
63	\small
64	\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}
65	\label{tab:instruct-qlora}
66	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
67	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
68	\toprule
69	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\
70	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}
71	\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\boldsymbol{\Delta}$ & \textbf{$N$} \\
72	\midrule
73	OLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\,$\pm$\,0.007 & $+$31.3\,pp & 4{,}429 \\
74	Qwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\,$\pm$\,0.008 & $+$17.5\,pp & 4{,}963 \\
75	\midrule
76	OLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\,$\pm$\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\
77	Qwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\,$\pm$\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\
78	\bottomrule
79	\end{tabular}}
80	\end{table}
81	
82	The XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.
83	Note that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.
84	All results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.

      "content": "\\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}\nTo test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-retrain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Base$\\rightarrow$Inst} & \\textbf{Retrained} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nRetraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).\nThree-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}\n$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.338 (vs.\\ 0.632 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.\nThe compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).\nCross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\\,=\\,0.490; instruct$\\rightarrow$base F1\\,=\\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.\nThese results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}\nTo test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-qlora}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\n\\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta}$ & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\\,$\\pm$\\,0.007 & $+$31.3\\,pp & 4{,}429 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\\,$\\pm$\\,0.008 & $+$17.5\\,pp & 4{,}963 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\\,$\\pm$\\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\\,$\\pm$\\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.\nNote that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.\n",

DDR shape stability → watermark preservation（future work）\n- **可行性**: 1-2 句 future work，无需实验。\n- **边际收益**: 低。无任何实验证据支撑——审稿人看到无证据的 future work 不会加分，只会标注 \"speculative\"。且 watermark 与 DDR 的关联需要严格论证（shape stability ≠ watermark 保持，两者作用在不同层面）。\n- **DDL 风险**: 极低（写作量极小）\n- **建议**: ❌ 不推荐。风险不在时间而在可信度——无证据的 watermark 连接容易被攻击为 hand-waving。Discussion 已经足够丰富，不需要靠未验证的推测填充。\n\n## W2：Base-model scope 限制\n\n### D. 1-card instruct DDR recovery 实验（adapted features）\n- **可行性**: 需重新设计特征集（现有 7 特征中 6/7 在 instruct 下 ρ 反转）、提取特征、训练、评估。GPU ~2-4h，但 pipeline 调试 + 分析 + 写入论文 ≥ 1 天。\n- **边际收益**: 低。核心问题不是特征设计——instruct DDR silhouette=0.338（vs base 0.632），说明 DDR 结构本身被压缩，不是特征选错了。即使 adapted features 提升了 within-model F1，cross-generator（LOGO）仍然无望（现有 3-gen instruct LOGO=0.487）。审稿人问的是 \"DDR 对 instruct 有效吗\"，答案是结构性 NO，adapted features 改不了这个答案。\n- **DDL 风险**: 中。5 天内做新实验 + 分析 + 修改多个章节，且大概率是 null result。\n- **建议**: ❌ 不推荐。instruct 已有充分数据证明 DDR 在该条件下降级——论文的正确策略是诚实标注边界，而不是试图挽救一个结构性弱点。\n\n### E. Instruct failure mode 深度分析（现有数据）\n- **可行性**: 无需 GPU。appendix_instruct.tex 已有丰富数据（ρ 反转、silhouette 压缩 0.632→0.338、cross-condition transfer 近 chance、QLoRA recovery +17.5-31.3pp）。\n- **边际收益**: 中。当前 Discussion L23 对 instruct 的处理只有 1 句（\"Instruction-tuned models show attenuated fingerprints...\"）。可以扩展为 2-3 句，将 appendix 中的关键发现提升到 Discussion：(1) alignment 不是消除 depth signal 而是将其压缩到 summary statistics 无法捕捉的层面（QLoRA 仍恢复到 0.89-0.91），(2) ρ 反转说明 alignment 重塑了 generation attractor 的方向而非强度。这让 \"base-model limitation\" 从被动承认变为主动分析——审稿人更接受 \"我们理解为什么它不 work\" 而非 \"它不 work 我们不管了\"。\n- **DDL 风险**: 极低（纯写作，30-60 min）\n- **建议**: ✅ 推荐。强化 Discussion instruct 段落，将 appendix 关键发现 promote。\n\n### F. Chat-format instruct chain 实验\n- **可行性**: 全新实验维度——需设计 chat-format 链生成 pipeline（system prompt + multi-turn）、生成数据、提取特征、训练评估。最低 ~8-16h GPU + pipeline 开发。\n- **边际收益**: 不确定且大概率低。chat-format 与 continuation chain 的生成机制差异更大（多轮对话 vs 单轮续写），DDR 可能完全不适用。即使成功也只是 \"又一个条件下 DDR 有效\"，不解决 instruct 核心问题。\n- **DDL 风险**: 极高。5 天内从零开始新实验维度，pipeline 调试风险大，null result 概率高。\n- **建议**: ❌ 不推荐。DDL 前 5 天启动全新实验维度违反基本 deadline 管理原则。\n\n---\n\n## 签字结论\n\n**推荐执行**（均为纯写作，总工时 ≤ 2h）：\n1. **A**: Discussion 扩展 corpus-level application 段落（含具体数字）\n2. **E**: Discussion 强化 instruct failure mode 分析（从 1 句扩展到 2-3 句）\n\n**不推荐**：B（被 A 覆盖）、C（无证据推测）、D（结构性问题非特征问题）、F（DDL 风险过高）\n\n**GPU 最终处置建议**：4 卡全部保留 standby，不派新实验。理由：\n- 推荐的 2 项行动均不需要 GPU\n- 距 DDL 5 天，GPU 的唯一价值是应急 re-run（如发现数据错误需重跑）\n- 无任何实验能在 5 天内改变论文的结构性弱点（base-model scope、medium novelty）\n\n**对目标评分的影响评估**：A+E 执行后，W1 \"so what\" 可从 \"明显弱点\" 降为 \"可接受的局限\"（corpus-level scenario 给了 practical grounding）；W2 base-model scope 不变但 framing 更诚实（审稿人更接受 well-understood limitation 而非 unexplained gap）。综合预期：维持 3.0-3.5 区间，A+E 的边际贡献约 +0.1-0.2，不足以跨过 3.5→4.0 的结构性门槛。",

}

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	Recursive generation alters \emph{where} the token-level surprise distribution sits but not \emph{how} it is shaped---a dichotomy that admits a mechanistic interpretation.
5	Under nucleus sampling, top-$p$ truncation selects from a fixed probability-mass fraction at each step, preserving the functional form of the conditional distribution regardless of input content.
6	What changes across recursive steps is the \emph{input} to this truncation: as the model conditions on its own increasingly self-typical outputs, the expected token-level NLL compresses monotonically---each generation feeds slightly more predictable input into the same shape-preserving mechanism.
7	This account yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift by disrupting mode-seeking, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it by anchoring the input distribution.
8	Both are empirically confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, the location signal collapses ($\rho$: $-0.60 \to -0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); under elaboration prompts, drift weakens relative to continuation (F1\,=\,0.730 vs.\ 0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}), consistent with \citet{broken_telephone}, while cross-type transfer remains viable (F1\,=\,0.753).
9	Permutation importance, Granger causality, and feature intervention analyses provide further causal evidence: drifting features contribute $5.5\times$ more to classification than near-stationary ones, with 6/7 features exhibiting significant Granger causality (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}).
10	
11	The classifier is a proof-of-concept, not a deployable system: document-level gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.63 and ${\sim}30\%$ error rate are insufficient for automated filtering but sufficient for corpus-level auditing ($O(1/\sqrt{n})$ error scaling), with the primary difficulty being gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ discrimination (86\% false negatives; Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).
12	Concretely, in a training-data curation pipeline, the classifier can estimate the proportion of recursively generated text in a candidate corpus: at $n{=}1{,}000$ documents, the gen-2+ fraction estimate has ${\sim}$$\pm$3\% precision under LOGO conditions, sufficient to flag batches exceeding a contamination threshold (e.g., $>$10\% gen-2+) for further review or exclusion.
13	This batch-level application sidesteps the per-document error rate that limits individual flagging.
14	In generator-unknown deployment, LOGO F1\,=\,0.694 provides a conservative lower bound (9.1\,pp per-generator spread); cross-temperature transfer degrades when test-time temperature exceeds training (F1\,$\to$\,0.723; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}).
15	
16	Preliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).
17	This scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.
18	
19	DDR connects to but is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining permanently erodes tail coverage by updating model weights, whereas inference-chain drift at fixed parameters shifts only the NLL distribution's location, leaving its shape as a stable reference.
20	Crucially, vocabulary impoverishment alone cannot explain DDR: if depth merely simplified the lexicon, shape features should also compress, yet entropy extremes and tail decay remain stable across all eight generators ($d < 0.10$; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
21	Moreover, semantic similarity between adjacent depths \emph{increases} monotonically (cosine: 0.62$\to$0.86), confirming that DDR captures statistical compression of the generation process rather than content degradation.
22	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}---diversity loss \cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \cite{markovian_chains}, mode collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion}---but not what is \emph{preserved}; this complementary observation is what enables depth detection without per-generator calibration.
23	Three depth classes represent the practical resolution ceiling (5-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.50; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), though QLoRA recovers substantially more signal (F1\,=\,0.905; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}); the $+$11.6\,pp gap reflects token-order dependencies and positional patterns that document-level aggregate statistics cannot capture.
24	Instruction-tuned models show attenuated fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct F1\,=\,0.338 vs.\ 0.763 base; DDR silhouette compresses from 0.632 to 0.338) with reversed feature correlations: alignment does not eliminate the depth signal but redirects it into representational dimensions that document-level summary statistics cannot capture.
25	This interpretation is supported by QLoRA's recovery to 0.89--0.91 on instruct data (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), confirming that the signal persists at the token-sequence level even when aggregate features lose discriminative power.
26	The base-model restriction is thus a limitation of the feature set, not of DDR as a phenomenon---a distinction relevant for future work targeting instruction-tuned chains.
27	
```

```text
Let me verify the claims in the new text against existing data:

**A (L12-13) - Corpus-level application:**
- "at n=1,000 documents, the gen-2+ fraction estimate has ~±3% precision under LOGO conditions"
- Need to verify this ±3% claim. With LOGO binary F1=0.694, what's the actual precision of corpus-level proportion estimation?
- The confusion matrix would give us the per-class precision/recall. From the error analysis appendix (mentioned in L11): gen-1 per-class F1=0.63, 86% false negatives for gen-1 vs gen-2+.


- For corpus-level proportion estimation, I need to account for how the classifier's observed positive rate translates to the true positive rate using the confusion matrix properties. The standard error scales with sample size and the classifier's discrimination ability. Given TPR around 0.70 and FPR around 0.30 for gen-2+, the ±3% margin at n=1000 seems plausible but I should verify this against the actual calibration curve.

- Working through the math: the adjustment factor of 2.5 amplifies the raw standard error from about 1.6% to 4% at 50% contamination, and down to 2.4% at 10% contamination, so ±3% sits reasonably in that range depending on the actual contamination level. This gives me confidence that the estimate is defensible for flagging batches above a 10% threshold.

- Now I'm checking the instruct failure mode numbers in the appendix. The base OLMo-2-1B shows F1 of 0.754 for the 3-class task, dropping to 0.575 when fine-tuned on instruct data, but I'm seeing references to 0.763 and 0.338 that don't match these values—they might be from a different evaluation setup like leave-one-out cross-validation. I'm noticing the 0.338 figure appears in multiple contexts—it could be from the LOGO evaluation or the instruct retraining results rather than the standard 5-fold cross-validation shown in the appendix table, which reports 0.575 for OLMo-Instruct instead. The 0.338 and 0.763 numbers might be from a different evaluation protocol—specifically LOGO evaluation mentioned in the appendix, which uses earlier generator subsets and wouldn't appear in the current table format. I should focus on verifying the new text rather than re-examining these pre-existing claims unless I find a clear inconsistency.

Looking at the modified L24-26, the key claims check out: the DDR silhouette compression from 0.632 to 0.338 matches the appendix, and the reversed feature correlations align with what's documented. The central new framing—that alignment redirects rather than eliminates the depth signal into representational dimensions—is the claim I need to verify against the evidence.

The QLoRA recovery to 0.89-0.91 on instruct data supports this interpretation well, showing the signal persists at the token level even when aggregate features degrade. The XGBoost performance drop (0.575, 0.732) confirms that aggregate features lose discriminative power, which strengthens the argument that the signal has been redirected rather than erased.

Now I'm checking whether the claim that DDR persists as a phenomenon under instruction-tuning is actually defensible. The evidence rests on QLoRA working on instruct data while XGBoost fails, but there's a critical methodological issue: QLoRA uses within-model cross-validation rather than leave-one-generator-out evaluation. The appendix shows that three-generator instruct LOGO yields only 0.487 binary F1—essentially chance performance—which means the DDR phenomenon's generalization across different instruction-tuned models isn't actually demonstrated. So the distinction between "base-model limitation" versus "DDR phenomenon limitation" is only supported within a single model, not cross-generator, which is an important caveat the paper glosses over. A more honest framing would be that the evidence is mixed—the token-level signal survives within-model (QLoRA shows recovery), but the cross-generator structure that makes DDR practically useful (LOGO performance) clearly degrades under instruct, and we can't yet separate whether that's a feature limitation or genuine weakening of the phenomenon itself. Now I'm checking whether the appendix actually contains the confusion matrix data needed to verify that ±3% claim.

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	Before analyzing the DDR decomposition (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}), we establish that generation depth is classifiable across diverse architectures.
8	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
9	Because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), gen-0 classification is near-perfect (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.94), inflating the 3-class metric.
10	We therefore adopt \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout: it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing.
11	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
14	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{5-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B); generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,2{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
15	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact: classifiers trained on one generator transfer to all others with mean binary F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO yields 0.686.
16	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
17	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier ($+$11.6\,pp, 3-class F1\,=\,0.905; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) confirms that richer representations capture additional depth signal; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the empirical decomposition---not the specific classifier---as DDR's primary contribution.
18	
19	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
20	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
21	
22	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
23	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both confirm the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
24	
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\footnotesize
28	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
29	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
30	\toprule
31	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-cl F1}$^{\dagger}$ \\
32	\midrule
33	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\
34	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \textbf{0.806} \\
35	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\
36	Llama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.697 & [.680, .713] & 0.775 \\
37	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & 0.781 \\
38	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.692 & [.674, .708] & 0.714 \\
39	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & 0.780 \\
40	Mistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.633 & [.617, .648] & 0.682 \\
41	\midrule

44	\bottomrule

46	\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
47	\label{tab:logo-results}
48	\end{table}
49	
50	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
51	\label{sec:results-differential}
52	
53	\begin{figure*}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
56	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}
57	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
58	\end{figure*}
59	
60	Not all distributional properties respond equally to recursive generation.
61	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a striking dichotomy that holds across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
62	We term this asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR).
63	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\,=\,0.632 (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \emph{drifting} features ($|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.499).
64	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
65	Five independent effect-size metrics converge on this drifting/non-drifting partition, confirming that the location/shape asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact.
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
70	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; three near-stationary features (amber; $d < 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled) is a boundary feature whose sensitivity concentrates at the gen-0/gen-1 transition.}
71	\label{fig:feature-drift}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	Pooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
75	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
76	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
77	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
78	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Quantification.}
81	Every tested effect-size metric confirms the split.
82	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} converges with $\rho$: drifting features show large effects ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) while shape features are near zero ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics show a ${\sim}10\times$ gap (0.516--0.537 vs.\ 0.043--0.052; all $p \approx 0$).
83	Glass's $\Delta$ and Cliff's $\delta$ corroborate the partition with a ${\geq}9.5\times$ gap; CV shift ($<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\%) supports ``near-stationary'' as a practical characterization (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
84	Five features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups; the remaining two require qualification.
85	Entropy min is a \emph{boundary feature}: its gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ effect is near-stationary ($d{=}0.016$) despite a substantial pooled $|\rho|$ (0.40) driven by the gen-0/gen-1 transition (Cliff's $\delta{=}0.354$ confirms shape change without location shift); $k{=}2$ clustering places it with non-drifting features (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
86	Entropy std shows a substantial level shift ($d{=}0.55$) despite low monotonicity ($|\rho|{=}0.16$), making it the sole intermediate feature.
87	\paragraph{Validation.}
88	The partition is not a pooling artifact.
89	Per-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) confirms stability for entropy min (8/8 generators pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 generators, with OLMo-2-1B ($d{=}0.22$) exceeding the equivalence bound (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}).
90	Bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 iterations) on the pooled sample achieves 100\% rejection for all three near-stationary features, but high pooled power ($N \approx 3{,}500$--$4{,}500$ per generator) can mask per-generator heterogeneity.
91	Tail decay's 50\% per-generator pass rate represents a genuine boundary case: reclassifying it as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and classification performance (F1 change $<$0.5\,pp).
92	
93	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
94	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
95	PCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\% variance; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\,pp, confirming that DDR's contribution is identifying \emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.
96	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
97	
98	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
99	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice.
100	
101	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
102	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).
103	Continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
104	
105	
106	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
107	\label{sec:results-baselines}
108	
109	Existing detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; their continuous scores encode distributional divergence that may incidentally correlate with recursive depth.
110	To test this, we repurpose four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
113	\centering
114	\footnotesize
115	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
116	\toprule
117	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-cl F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
118	\midrule
119	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
120	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
121	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
122	\midrule
123	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
124	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
125	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
126	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
127	\bottomrule
128	\end{tabular}
129	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; slightly higher than per-generator macro average in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
130	\label{tab:baselines}
131	\end{table}
132	
133	\begin{figure}[t]
134	\centering
135	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
136	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble matches multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth.}
137	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
138	\end{figure}
139	
140	The 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,6.7--36.3\,pp; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\ 0.700).
141	Adding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\times$ the inference cost.
142	This result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance---where multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it---but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
143

Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. $^{\\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}).}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nNot all distributional properties respond equally to recursive generation.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a striking dichotomy that holds across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.\nWe term this asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR).\nThe primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.632 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \\emph{drifting} features ($|\\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \\emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \\leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\\,=\\,0.499).\nA domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \\emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\\% vs.\\ $>$15\\% for drifting features).\nFive independent effect-size metrics converge on this drifting/non-drifting partition, confirming that the location/shape asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; three near-stationary features (amber; $d < 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled) is a boundary feature whose sensitivity concentrates at the gen-0/gen-1 transition.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the binary split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.60$),\nwhile non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-distributions}).\nThe six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\\rho| \\leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.\nWithin the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\nEvery tested effect-size metric confirms the split.\nCohen's $d$ \\cite{cohen1988statistical} converges with $\\rho$: drifting features show large effects ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) while shape features are near zero ($d \\leq 0.08$); KS statistics show a ${\\sim}10\\times$ gap (0.516--0.537 vs.\\ 0.043--0.052; all $p \\approx 0$).\nGlass's $\\Delta$ and Cliff's $\\delta$ corroborate the partition with a ${\\geq}9.5\\times$ gap; CV shift ($<$3\\% vs.\\ $>$15\\%) supports ``near-stationary'' as a practical characterization (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nFive features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups; the remaining two require qualification.\nEntropy min is a \\emph{boundary feature}: its gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ effect is near-stationary ($d{=}0.016$) despite a substantial pooled $|\\rho|$ (0.40) driven by the gen-0/gen-1 transition (Cliff's $\\delta{=}0.354$ confirms shape change without location shift); $k{=}2$ clustering places it with non-drifting features (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nEntropy std shows a substantial level shift ($d{=}0.55$) despite low monotonicity ($|\\rho|{=}0.16$), making it the sole intermediate feature.\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nThe partition is not a pooling artifact.\nPer-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) confirms stability for entropy min (8/8 generators pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 generators, with OLMo-2-1B ($d{=}0.22$) exceeding the equivalence bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\nBootstrap resampling (1{,}000 iterations) on the pooled sample achieves 100\\% rejection for all three near-stationary features, but high pooled power ($N \\approx 3{,}500$--$4{,}500$ per generator) can mask per-generator heterogeneity.\nTail decay's 50\\% per-generator pass rate represents a genuine boundary case: reclassifying it as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and classification performance (F1 change $<$0.5\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nPCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\\% variance; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\\,pp, confirming that DDR's contribution is identifying \\emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \\cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\nAll four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).\nContinuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nExisting detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; their continuous scores encode distributional divergence that may incidentally correlate with recursive depth.\nTo test this, we repurpose four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; slightly higher than per-generator macro average in Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble matches multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,6.7--36.3\\,pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\\ 0.700).\nAdding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\\times$ the inference cost.\nThis result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance---where multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it---but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n",

```text
Now I have all the data I need. Let me verify the key claims in the new text:

**A (L12-13): Corpus-level application**

L12: "at n=1,000 documents, the gen-2+ fraction estimate has ~±3% precision under LOGO conditions"

From the confusion matrix (appendix_confusion_matrix.tex):
- True gen-2+ → Pred gen-2+: 73.7% (TPR for gen-2+)
- True gen-1 → Pred gen-2+: 18.8% (FPR from gen-1)


- True gen-0 → Pred gen-2+: 0.0% (FPR from gen-0)

For corpus-level proportion estimation of gen-2+, I need to calculate the effective false positive rate across all non-gen-2+ classes. The TPR is about 0.737, and I'm working through the FPR calculation by looking at how many non-gen-2+ documents get misclassified as gen-2+.

From the confusion matrix, gen-0 and gen-1 together make up 11,890 non-gen-2+ documents, of which 1,113 are predicted as gen-2+, giving an FPR of roughly 0.094. Now I'm setting up the bias-corrected proportion estimation formula, where I can recover the true proportion from the observed positive rate using the TPR and FPR to adjust for classification errors.

With TPR around 0.737 and FPR at 0.094, the difference is 0.643. I'm testing what the observed positive rate would be under different true prevalence scenarios—if the true gen-2+ rate is 10%, the observed rate would be about 15.8%, with a standard error around 1.15% and a corrected 95% confidence interval of roughly ±3.5%. For a 20% true rate, the calculation continues similarly. So at n=1000 with a 5% true contamination rate, the confidence interval narrows to about ±3.2%, which aligns reasonably well with the "~±3%" claim, though it's slightly optimistic at higher contamination rates. The bigger limitation here is that this approach treats the confusion matrix values as fixed and known, when in reality there's uncertainty in the TPR and FPR estimates themselves. Plus, a simple classify-and-count method isn't the most sophisticated approach to quantification.

The "~±3% precision" claim holds up for lower contamination rates around 5-10%, making it acceptable with the qualifier, though it overshoots a bit at higher rates. For the batch-level application at a 10% threshold, this precision is workable for screening purposes—you'd catch batches genuinely exceeding ~14% gen-2+ but might miss ones right at the threshold. The conceptual point about sidestepping per-document error rates is sound.

Now looking at the instruct model analysis, comparing OLMo-Instruct's F1 score of 0.338 against...

Actually, let me reconsider the alignment claim. The evidence shows that summary statistics lose discriminative power while token-level models recover to 0.89-0.91, which supports the idea that the depth signal persists but shifts into representational space. Though "redirects" is my interpretation—it could equally mean the signal weakens but token-level capacity still captures what remains. The QLoRA recovery numbers check out against the appendix.

Now I'm scrutinizing the claim that base-model restriction is a feature-set limitation rather than a DDR phenomenon limitation. The QLoRA recovery and feature reversal suggest the signal survives alignment, but the evidence cuts both ways: the DDR structure itself degrades sharply (silhouette 0.632→0.338), cross-generator transfer fails badly (F1=0.487), and cross-condition generalization barely beats chance (0.490). So while the signal might persist in some form, the clean drifting/non-drifting partition that defines DDR as a phenomenon is genuinely compromised—that's not just a feature-set problem. The issue is that claiming "DDR as a phenomenon persists" misrepresents what the evidence shows — the silhouette metric (0.632 → 0.338) directly measures the location/shape partition degrading under instruction tuning, which is DDR itself degrading. QLoRA's token-level recovery only proves some depth signal survives, not that the specific drifting/non-drifting decomposition structure remains intact. The stronger, more accurate framing is that alignment degrades the DDR structure while leaving residual depth information that QLoRA can exploit, even if through different mechanisms entirely. Now I'm putting together the verification report.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Director is pushing back on my "no experiments worth running" conclusion and raising a specific hypothesis I didn't consider: reference model mismatch. Let me think about this carefully.

The hypothesis: When computing NLL features for instruct model outputs, we use Pythia-410M (a base model) as the reference. Instruct outputs have different distributional properties than base outputs (due to RLHF/alignment). A base reference model might assign systematically different NLL distributions to instruct outputs in a way that compresses inter-depth differences. Using an instruct reference model (Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct) might recover some DDR signal.


Actually, this is plausible. A base reference model could be assigning uniformly high or low surprise to patterns introduced by alignment—like safety phrases or instruction-following structures—which would systematically bias the NLL features and mask any depth-dependent variation. An instruct-aligned reference model would better capture those residual depth signals since it understands the distributional properties of instruct-style text. The Director caught something real that I overlooked.

Now I'm weighing two concrete options: creating a visualization of the DDR partition across base versus instruct models, which would be straightforward and strengthen the appendix, versus running the instruct reference experiment where I'd swap in a smaller instruct model, re-extract NLL features, and compare the DDR metrics to see if the reference mismatch was actually suppressing the signal. The second one is more of an analysis task than a training task, so it's feasible on limited compute. The key question is whether an instruct reference would partially recover the depth signal and what that would tell us about the original findings. Scenarios:
a) Full recovery (silhouette back to ~0.5+, ρ reversal corrected): Would be a significant finding — shows instruct limitation is methodological, not fundamental. Major addition to instruct appendix, possibly warrants mention in Discussion.

b) Partial recovery (e.g., silhouette 0.338→0.45, some ρ values improve): Interesting data point. Shows reference mismatch contributes to instruct degradation but isn't the whole story. Moderate addition to appendix.

c) No recovery (silhouette stays ~0.338, ρ still reversed): Also informative — rules out reference mismatch as explanation, strengthens the "alignment fundamentally reshapes attractor" interpretation.

d) Worsening: Unlikely but would also be informative.

The core question is whether this experiment shifts the paper's narrative. If I get full recovery, I could reframe the instruct limitation from a fundamental constraint to a practical one about matched reference models — a more actionable takeaway for the Discussion. Partial recovery adds nuance to the boundary analysis, while no recovery confirms the current framing. Given the tight timeline with 5 days left, running feature extraction through a 0.6B model should take 2-4 hours total, making this feasible. I'd document the results primarily in the instruct appendix, with potential Discussion updates if the findings are strong enough.

The Director's point about reference model mismatch was a blind spot I should have caught earlier when evaluating option D. Now I'm thinking through the GPU requirements — inference on a single card through Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct is lightweight and should finish in under an hour. Time to draft my response.
```

0	\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}
1	
2	Table~\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.
3	All results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.
4	
5	\begin{table}[ht]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}
9	\label{tab:instruct}
10	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
11	\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}
12	\toprule
13	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Binary F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\boldsymbol{\rho}$\textbf{(ent.\ mean)}} & \\
14	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
15	\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \textbf{Rev.} \\
16	\midrule
17	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
18	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
19	Llama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	Instruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\Delta| \leq 3.2$\,pp).
25	The asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.
26	Entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\ 3 of 7 for Llama.
27	This pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}

Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}",

```text
Perfect! I now have all the information needed. Let me compile the findings:

## Key Findings: Pythia-410M Reference Model in NLL Feature Extraction

### 1. **Methodology Section (method.tex)**

**Reference Model Description:**
- Pythia-410M is explicitly described as "a held-out reference model... chosen to be smaller than all generators"
- It computes **per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL)** for each text
- The paper uses "entropy" as shorthand for token-level NLL values (following convention in prior detection work), though these are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution
- Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, the methodology explicitly states that "cross-family reference model experiments confirm that depth features are not reference-specific"

**Five NLL-derived features extracted:**
1. **entropy_mean**: mean token-level NLL (average predictability)
2. **entropy_std**: standard deviation of token-level NLL (spread of per-token surprise)
3. **entropy_max**: maximum token-level NLL (most surprising token)
4. **entropy_min**: minimum token-level NLL (confidence floor on most predictable token)
5. **tail_decay**: slope of vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in mid-rank region (ranks 100-1000), capturing probability mass decay

### 2. **Appendix on Cross-Family Reference Models (table4.tex / app:cross-ref)**

Three alternative reference models tested with results:
- **Pythia-410M** (Pythia family): LOGO F1 = **0.803**, Entropy Drift = [-1.19, -1.40]
- **OLMo-2-1B** (OLMo family): LOGO F1 = **0.807**, Entropy Drift = [-0.61, -0.91]
- **Qwen3-0.6B** (Qwen family): LOGO F1 = **0.816**, Entropy Drift = [-0.96, -1.35]

**Key Finding:** Drift direction is consistent (negative) across all three references; magnitude varies with capacity. The spread in LOGO F1 is **≤ 0.013**, confirming that depth features are robust to reference model choice.

### 3. **Results.tex Lines 98-100 (Cross-Family Reference Models)**

Line 99 reports: "Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread **0.013**; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice."

Additionally, line 16 notes that Pythia achieves F1 of 0.718-0.724 while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread ≤ 0.013).
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is very useful. Let me now think about the reference model mismatch hypothesis more carefully.

From the cross-reference appendix:
- 3 base reference models tested: Pythia-410M, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B
- All base models, all show consistent drift direction (negative ρ)
- LOGO F1 spread ≤ 0.013 — tiny variation

The Director's hypothesis: using an **instruct** reference model for **instruct** chain outputs might recover signal.

The logic:
1. Current setup: base reference (Pythia-410M) → instruct chain outputs → NLL features → ρ reversal, silhouette compression


2. Proposed: instruct reference (Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct) → instruct chain outputs → NLL features → ρ recovery?

The cross-ref experiment shows base-to-base swaps don't matter much, but switching to an instruct reference is fundamentally different. An instruct model has learned different token distributions and would treat instruct-style patterns—safety disclaimers, structured responses—as more typical, assigning lower baseline surprise to these constructs. This could reshape the NLL distribution in ways that actually recover the correlation signal.

The hypothesis is scientifically sound as a confound: a base reference systematically penalizes instruct text, potentially masking depth trends, while an instruct reference would provide more calibrated scores where those trends could emerge. But there's a wrinkle—the cross-ref work already showed that different base model families produce similar depth-dependent reversal patterns despite architectural differences, and the base-to-instruct shift is much larger than base-to-base family shifts. So the reference mismatch is clearly a real effect, even if it's not the whole story.

Now I'm thinking through what happens if the instruct reference experiment actually works. If the reversal corrects and the silhouette improves to around 0.4-0.5, that's a major finding—it means the instruct failure is partly a methodological artifact, not a fundamental limitation. That would let me reframe the whole thing as "DDR needs matched references for instruct models" and open a clear path forward. If nothing recovers, at least I've ruled out reference mismatch as the culprit and strengthened the interpretation that alignment genuinely reshapes the attractor landscape.

The computational cost is minimal—just a forward pass on the existing instruct chain data with the smaller Qwen model, probably 15-30 minutes on an RTX 5090. There's almost no deadline risk either since feature extraction is automated and the analysis is straightforward. I need to write this up honestly though, acknowledging that the Director caught a real blind spot in my original thinking about reference model mismatch, and the cross-reference experiment I ran only tested base models.
```

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nextract_features_instruct_ref.py — Extract NLL features using Qwen3-0.6B (instruct) as reference model.\\n\\nPurpose: Test whether an instruct reference model recovers DDR signal on instruct-generated chains,\\ncompared to the base Pythia-410M reference model.\\n\\nInput: data/{qwen3-1.7b-instruct,olmo-2-0425-1b-instruct}/generations_shard0.jsonl\\nOutput: data/features_instruct_ref_qwen06b.csv\\n        columns: generator, id, depth, seed_id, entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_min, entropy_max,\\n                 tail_decay, ttr, hapax_ratio\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nimport argparse\\nimport json\\nimport sys\\nfrom collections import Counter\\nfrom pathlib import Path\\n\\nimport numpy as np\\nimport pandas as pd\\nimport torch\\nfrom transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\\n\\nMODEL_PATH = \\\"/root/autodl-tmp/models/qwen3-0.6b-instruct/Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B\\\"\\n\\nGENERATORS = {\\n    \\\"qwen3-1.7b-instruct\\\": \\\"data/qwen3-1.7b-instruct/generations_shard0.jsonl\\\",\\n    \\\"olmo-2-0425-1b-instruct\\\": \\\"data/olmo-2-0425-1b-instruct/generations_shard0.jsonl\\\",\\n}\\n\\n\\ndef load_data(input_path: str) -> list[dict]:\\n    records = []\\n    with open(input_path) as f:\\n        for line in f:\\n            line = line.strip()\\n            if line:\\n                records.append(json.loads(line))\\n    print(f\\\"  Loaded {len(records)} records from {input_path}\\\")\\n    return records\\n\\n\\ndef load_model(device: str):\\n    print(f\\\"Loading reference model from {MODEL_PATH} on {device}\\\")\\n    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_PATH, local_files_only=True)\\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\\n        MODEL_PATH, local_files_only=True, torch_dtype=torch.float16,\\n    ).to(device).eval()\\n    if tokenizer.pad_token is None:\\n        tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token\\n    print(\\\"Model loaded\\\")\\n    return tokenizer, model\\n\\n\\ndef compute_reference_features(texts, tokenizer, model, device, batch_size):\\n    results = []\\n    for start in range(0, len(texts), batch_size):\\n        batch_texts = texts[start:start + batch_size]\\n        encodings = tokenizer(\\n            batch_texts, return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\", padding=True,\\n            truncation=True, max_length=512,\\n        ).to(device)\\n\\n        with torch.no_grad():\\n            logits = model(**encodings).logits\\n\\n        for i in range(len(batch_texts)):\\n            mask = encodings[\\\"attention_mask\\\"][i].bool()\\n            seq_len = mask.sum().item()\\n            if seq_len < 2:\\n                results.append(dict(entropy_mean=0., entropy_std=0., entropy_min=0.,\\n                                    entropy_max=0., tail_decay=0.))\\n                continue\\n\\n            token_logits = logits[i, :seq_len - 1, :]\\n            log_probs = torch.log_softmax(token_logits, dim=-1)\\n            probs = torch.exp(log_probs)\\n            entropy = -(probs * log_probs).sum(dim=-1)\\n            entropy_np = entropy.float().cpu().numpy()\\n\\n            avg_log_probs = log_probs.float().mean(dim=0)\\n            sorted_lp, _ = torch.sort(avg_log_probs, descending=True)\\n            sorted_lp_np = sorted_lp.cpu().numpy()\\n\\n            vocab_size = sorted_lp_np.shape[0]\\n            hi = min(1000, vocab_size)\\n            lo = min(100, hi)\\n            if hi - lo >= 2:\\n                x = np.arange(lo, hi, dtype=np.float64)\\n                y = sorted_lp_np[lo:hi].astype(np.float64)\\n                coeffs = np.polyfit(x, y, 1)\\n                tail_decay = float(coeffs[0])\\n            else:\\n                tail_decay = 0.0\\n\\n            results.append(dict(\\n                entropy_mean=float(np.mean(entropy_np)),\\n                entropy_std=float(np.std(entropy_np)),\\n                entropy_min=float(np.min(entropy_np)),\\n                entropy_max=float(np.max(entropy_np)),\\n                tail_decay=tail_decay,\\n            ))\\n\\n        if (start // batch_size) % 20 == 0:\\n            print(f\\\"    {min(start + batch_size, len(texts))}/{len(texts)}\\\")\\n\\n    return results\\n\\n\\ndef compute_surface_features(texts):\\n    results = []\\n    for text in texts:\\n        words = text.lower().split()\\n        n = len(words)\\n        if n == 0:\\n            results.append(dict(ttr=0., hapax_ratio=0.))\\n            continue\\n        counter = Counter(words)\\n        results.append(dict(\\n            ttr=len(counter) / n,\\n            hapax_ratio=sum(1 for c in counter.values() if c == 1) / n,\\n        ))\\n    return results\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--batch-size\\\", type=int, default=8)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--device\\\", default=\\\"cuda:0\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--dry-run\\\", action=\\\"store_true\\\")\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    project_root = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent\\n\\n    tokenizer, model = load_model(args.device)\\n\\n    all_rows = []\\n    for gen_name, rel_path in GENERATORS.items():\\n        input_path = project_root / rel_path\\n        print(f\\\"\\\\nProcessing {gen_name}...\\\")\\n        records = load_data(str(input_path))\\n        if args.dry_run:\\n            records = records[:10]\\n            print(f\\\"  [dry-run] Using 10 records\\\")\\n\\n        texts = [r[\\\"text\\\"] for r in records]\\n\\n        ref_feats = compute_reference_features(texts, tokenizer, model, args.device, args.batch_size)\\n        surf_feats = compute_surface_features(texts)\\n\\n        for rec, ref, surf in zip(records, ref_feats, surf_feats):\\n            all_rows.append(dict(\\n                generator=gen_name,\\n                id=rec[\\\"id\\\"],\\n                depth=rec[\\\"depth\\\"],\\n                seed_id=rec[\\\"seed_id\\\"],\\n                **ref, **surf,\\n            ))\\n\\n    del model, tokenizer\\n    torch.cuda.empty_cache()\\n\\n    df = pd.DataFrame(all_rows)\\n    out_path = project_root / \\\"data\\\" / \\\"features_instruct_ref_qwen06b.csv\\\"\\n    df.to_csv(out_path, index=False)\\n    print(f\\\"\\\\nSaved {df.shape} to {out_path}\\\")\\n    print(df.groupby(\\\"generator\\\").describe().T)\\n\\n\\nif __name__ == \\\"__main__\\\":\\n    main()\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.37}}"}

entropy_std, entropy_min, entropy_max,\\n                 tail_decay, ttr, hapax_ratio\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nimport argparse\\nimport json\\nimport sys\\nfrom collections import Counter\\nfrom pathlib ).to(device).eval()\\n    if tokenizer.pad_token is None:\\n        tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token\\n    print(\\\"Model loaded\\\")\\n    return tokenizer, model\\n\\n\\ndef compute_reference_features(texts, tokenizer, model, device, batch_size):\\n    results = []\\n    for start in range(0, len(texts), batch_size):\\n        batch_texts = texts[start:start + min(1000, vocab_size)\\n            lo = min(100, hi)\\n            if hi - lo >= 2:\\n                x = np.arange(lo, hi, dtype=np.float64)\\n                y =
